Chapter 201

“...!!”

Baek Saheon suddenly jerked his head up.

Just now—there had been a °• N o v e l i g h t •° tremendous noise behind him. Something even more serious might have happened in the village....

“Fuck, is this really the time to be thinking about that?”

He grit his teeth and started running again.

He had to get as far away as possible. Fast.

“Huff!”

The enormous backpack Baek Saheon carried as he bolted down the mountain path was filled with water, food, and carefully chosen survival supplies.

He had stashed it in a secret location long ago, in case he ever had to flee the village on short notice. It was hard to believe he was finally using it.

But in the leftover empty space inside the pack...

...was a set of bones.

The remnants left behind when the pungmul band collapsed and vanished.

Among them, the bones of the one who had once been his sister.

“......”

He should’ve left them behind, they were heavy.

He knew it was a stupid thing to do, and still—he couldn’t stop himself from bringing them. And now that they were in the bag, he couldn’t just toss them out onto the ground.

That, at least, was the only thing on his mind.

“I wish they’d all just die.”

Good riddance.

The villagers, the outsiders who came, even the agents who’d failed to save him—

He wished they’d all die, and this fucking village would disappear without a trace.

The very idea gave him a heady thrill that tingled all the way up his spine.

But...

“...Still, that agent...”

The bespectacled young agent who had been tricked into drawing the “Special Prize.”

The one who stayed calm, who never once mocked or got angry with him. Who helped him, without ever demanding anything in return.

And in the end—kept his word.

That they would both survive.

That he wouldn’t betray him.

“......”

A strange feeling stirred inside him.

It was the first time in his life he’d felt something like this.

The sensation that came from doing something good for once—on a whim, and accomplishing it.

People called it: “a sense of fulfillment.”

“......”

That must’ve been why. Why he’d left that little folded paper boat behind.

Of course, it would only mean anything if that agent had survived the nightmarish centipede-and-cleaver mess and found it.

“...If he picks it up, I’ll think about it then.”

Given all the shit that happened with the centipede, the cleavers, and everything else—he might already be dead by now....

At that moment—

“......!”

A chilling, electric pressure crept down his nape like a blade—terrifying and cold.

Something was behind him.

Something strange was definitely still happening in the village.

“Fuck.”

In the end, Baek Saheon couldn’t help it—he glanced back.

And then—

“......!!”

His face went pale.

He broke into a full sprint again, down the mountain trail.

He never looked back again.

“Fuck.”

The scene he had just witnessed—

Red blood was raining down from the sky.

I was lying face down.

It happened instinctively. My body dropped flat to the ground, both hands planted on the dirt.

As if in a bow.

I see you

I see you

I see you

Drip, drip.

Warm blood poured down onto my head and the backs of my hands.

Filthy beast

Pestilence

Rotten fox spirit

A searing pain ignited in one hand—my left. The one that hadn't been severed.

It felt like I was going to die.

It stinks of rot

I clawed at the ground with my hand.

“Agent Podo!”

I heard Agent Cheongdong and Agent Choi arguing, somewhere nearby.

“That’s Daechongbong’s guardian spirit, isn’t it?! Have you lost your mind?! That’s a supernatural entity not even friendly to humans—!”

“Later! Quiet for now!”

I gritted my teeth and looked sideways.

The Ho Director wearing Deputy Eunhaje’s face was...

Let me see you

Filthy, low creature

Smiling as blood rained down on him.

“This one is my acquaintance. Elder, do excuse us and go about your business.”

Blisters boiled up on the back of my left hand.

I see a tail

“This one is my acquaintance. Elder, why don’t you shout from beyond the threshold?”

The skin burst and pus bloomed forth. A mark of plague.

The tail is severed

“This one is my acquaintance. Elder, why don’t you sit on the porch and enjoy a meal?”

The abscess ruptured, oozing.

Deceitful tongue

The body wearing Deputy Eunhaje’s face began to float into the air.

“......!”

Its limbs twisted grotesquely as it was suspended—but the gentle smile never left its face.

“Don—”

“Shh.”

Agent Choi placed a hand on my shoulder.

Die.

Thunder cracked.

Leave only your hide

Burn to death.

“...No thanks.”

Through the lightning, I saw Ho’s expression.

“To leave only your skin when you die—that’s a tiger, not me. What are you even talking about?”

He was still smiling.

“‘A little knowledge is a dangerous thing,’ they say. How frightening. But I’m no fox spirit. Do you have any proof I’m a fox? Proof I’m a spirit? Should we test it? Begone, evil ghost—Namu Amitabul Kwanseum Bosal, gupgup yeoryeong, Our Father who art in Heaven—throw some salt, scatter some red beans.”

Lightning again.

“But it doesn’t work, does it?”

A tearing sound.

“What a shame. You must have been mistaken... how tragic.”

That serene voice continued, even as the body hovered in midair.

“You can’t burn me.”

From the rain of blood above, a gigantic eye appeared.

Noisy little thing

Ho’s limbs contorted unnaturally.

How long you’ve lingered

How foul you’ve become

Begone, filth.

“Well, now.”

Grab its tail

And run.

“Really quite cruel of you. To forcefully separate me from my companion like this.”

The smiling face turned toward me.

“What a pity... Soleum-nim.”

“...!”

And in the very next instant—

It vanished.

The body of Deputy Eunhaje, bound and grotesquely suspended in midair, was gone—like it had never been there to begin with.

The entity possessing that body—Ho Director—disappeared along with it.

“......”

“......”

Did he run away...?

My mind was already spinning with tangled thoughts. How exactly was Deputy Eunhaje being controlled? What kind of prohibition was he under? Is he okay now?

But at the same time, I found myself looking up at the sky.

The blood rain had stopped.

I looked back down at my hand.

The blisters and pus were completely gone.

As if they’d been washed away by that very blood.

Filth removed.

As promised.

“......”

“Wait, General! Didn’t you say you’d make that fellow grovel and offer a full apology? He ran off just fine though?”

Silence, insolent one.

Agent Choi was yanked upward, suspended midair by the neck.

“Agent-nim!”

“Ugh—hahaha...”

I quickly looked up and shouted.

“I’m sorry! And... thank you!”

......

A child who knows respect.

Something from above... looked down on me.

Good.

It observed me.

And declared:

Escort him.

...Wait.

Offer your bow.

Permission granted.

Something—something strange began to press on my shoulders and back.

A force... gently compelling me to bow.

“No, thank you.”

“......”

Agent Choi raised his hand.

“We already serve one guardian here. Can you see? The Goblin Elder.”

From the tip of his hand, the goblin lantern flickered.

“Now that the procession is over, you should go enjoy that feast we prepared. Before it gets cold, please—return, Tiger General.”

Cheeky brat.

A low rumble sounded—like a big cat snorting.

Then serve me the feast

As promised.

And with that...

All was still.

“......”

The blood rain from the sky, the lightning crashing down—it all vanished, like a lie.

“Whew...”

With a collective exhale, all three agents dropped to the ground.

I finally managed to speak.

“That presence just now—”

“Oh, right. That was the Seoraksan Daechongbong Guardian Tiger-General Bodhisattva. Bit of a mouthful, but you can think of him that way. Seems he prefers being called that.”

Ah.

That moment, I understood what kind of folklore this was.

Daechongbong Tiger General

A tiger ghost tale derived from Baekhodae Sal, a calamitous spirit in traditional Korean horoscopes said to bring bloodshed and punishment.

But according to the Paranormal Disaster Response Agency, if such a presence is served and appeased correctly, it becomes a guardian that devours misfortune instead.

(For detailed mission records, see: Archive 11.93C - Daechongbong Pilgrimage.)

And that meant...

You must climb Daechongbong of Seoraksan, alone and unaided, bearing an offering on your back.

That’s what Agent Choi had done.

“......”

I fought to keep myself from asking about it, forcefully changing the subject.

“According to Agent Cheongdong, it wasn’t a particularly human-friendly supernatural entity...”

“Oh, that.”

Agent Choi shrugged.

“Well... more like, it just doesn’t care. But if you serve the feast properly, he’ll usually hear you out—if he’s in a good mood.”

“Even if the feast includes humans?”

Agent Cheongdong’s voice burned with outrage. Agent Choi chuckled.

“Ehh, headquarters is doing a good job sweet-talking him. He’s doing the whole vegan thing these days.”

And then—

His eyes flicked instantly to me.

Or more precisely, to the back of my hand—now clean from the blood rain.

There was a faint tension in his gaze, barely hidden.

“It’s gone... right?”

“......”

Agent Cheongdong’s eyes were also fixed on my hand.

After what they’d seen earlier—how that thing had possessed the deputy’s body—it wouldn’t be surprising if they suspected something had latched onto me too.

“Agent Podo, what the hell...”

“We’ll talk when we get back. First, we need to confirm it. ...Podo. Say anything. Just speak.”

I hesitated, then opened my mouth.

“...I’m a deer.”

“......!”

It worked.

No resistance at all.

It was done.

“It’s done!”

The curse was broken.

Holy shit.

I stared dumbfounded at my own hand.

And just then—from beyond the village—a bicycle bell began to ring.

“Ju-jak Squad’s here.”

The signal that it was over.

“This village of Jisan... still reeks of strange things. They’ll begin a proper sweep now. Start looking for people. Investigate that well too.”

Agent Choi casually knocked on the well’s edge.

“There might still be more weird shit buried deep down, but... not today. Let’s go home.”

“...Yes.”

A strange relief spread through my chest.

Yeah. The curse was gone.

No more countdown to death in thirty days.

I was free—

......

Wait.

What a pity, Soleum-nim.

“...Agent Podo?”

I was wrong.

I’d been swept up in emotion. Let myself relax over something ridiculous. This was no time for relief.

I needed to calm down.

“Fuck.”

I couldn’t afford to fall out with Ho Director here.

Sure, dying in thirty days if I failed the mission was a big deal—but what was bigger was succeeding in that mission and getting the Wish Token.

If things went this way, it would be too obvious a betrayal. If I wanted to keep going...

No. This wouldn’t do.

I turned to look at Agent Choi.

Did he... plan for this? For me to fall out with Ho Director completely?

“......”

“Podo.”

He didn’t meet my eyes.

Still smiling, Agent Choi unsheathed his cleaver and began inspecting the blade.

As he said,

“By the way, before we go back—there’s a place Podo needs to stop by. Standard Agency procedure.”

“...Huh?”

The cleaver spun once in his hand.

“You were cursed by a prohibition even the Agency didn’t know about.”

“...!”

“So we have to investigate. Figure out what happened. It’s protocol. For your safety... in containment.”

Along with the blade, something else came into view.

A small glass orb.

“Let’s go.”

The same thing I’d seen days ago.

The portable glass prison.

No.

“Wa—”

Thud.

“......!!”

Before I could even finish my sentence, hands burst forth from the void around the orb and grabbed me.

“Agent—!”

“Shh.”

Caught completely off guard—I had no chance to resist. I was restrained in seconds.

“If you cooperate with the inquiry, you’ll be out in no time. Don’t worry.”

I grit my teeth and stared at him.

Agent Choi blocked Agent Cheongdong’s dash forward with his blade.

“He’s mostly back to normal. It’ll be fine.”

“Agent Choi, what are you—”

“Just wait.”

He turned back to me, voice soft.

“Rest a bit... Podo.”

And I was pulled into the void.

Transported—into the glass prison.

Chapter 202

Unknown subterranean location.

A steel-reinforced zone, dark and cold, like a bunker tunnel.

“......”

“This way, Agent-nim.”

The agent from Hyunmu Team 1 followed the escort.

The glass prison of the Paranormal Disaster Response Agency had no human guards.

An unseen, soundless, shapeless supernatural force served as its warden. And to monitor that “warden,” a designated staffer was rotated on a strict schedule.

That staffer now asked, cautiously:

“...Are you here for the interrogation of Agent Podo—no, 37-999?”

“......”

“I see. Right this way.”

The agent passed through a thick metal door, moving carefully to avoid disturbing the ‘warden.’ On the other side was—

A crystal cave.

“...!”

Quartz clusters refracted light across the cavern, casting countless shadows and figures that flickered and morphed on every surface.

And yet, no source of that light could be seen.

A cascade of illumination and distortion with no origin.

Click.

“This is the holding cell.”

The staffer opened a rusted door near the far wall.

But there was no figure inside.

Instead...

The walls were filled with orbs.

Glass spheres, stacked in a tapering spiral, reflected the shimmering light.

To an uninformed eye, it may have looked ornamental.

But inside each orb—movement.

“Aaaah...”

“Let me out let me out let me out please—”

“You betrayed me.”

“Is this where I die?”

Isolated detainees.

Standing before them, one felt like a giant, able to observe each orb and its captive up close.

A twisted panopticon—and the true nature of the glass prison.

Each orb, a solitary confinement cell.

No known map led to this crystalline maze. Without the escort of the gatekeeper, no detainee could be moved.

Even if a prisoner managed to shatter their own orb and escape—they would only find themselves inside another.

A closed loop. An endless sequence of rooms made of glass.

“This is the topmost cell. I was told it was a special request. Something about hoping it’d be a little more comfortable... Since they were an agent, once.”

The agent’s eyes stopped.

Exactly 37 levels up.

At the very summit, there was only one orb.

Its designation:

37-999

“......”

A dark silhouette could be seen inside, lying still on the floor.

Not moving.

“I’ve gone on too long. Apologies. Here’s the last interrogation report for 37-999.”

The agent took the file with a stiff hand and began reading.

Inmate Number: 37-999

Detainee: Kim ■■ (Agent Codename: Podo)

Affiliation: Emergency Rescue Squad, Hyunmu Team 1 (Suspended)

Initially transferred for a 24-hour routine psychological evaluation. However, due to abnormal developments during the first interview, their release has been delayed.

Formal detainment under discussion.

The agent’s grip tightened slightly on that last line, but then they turned the page.

Day 1.

Transferred to the glass prison for post-curse evaluation. The prior curse—confirmed to have been dispelled (origin: Daechongbong Tiger-General).

Basic interview and physical check proceeded.

※ During interview, anomaly discovered.

Subject refused to answer any questions regarding the identity, time, or nature of the supernatural entity that had cursed them.

Evaluation halted. Interrogation initiated.

At first, the questioning had been gentle.

Why the refusal to answer? Any injury? Any guesses on when the curse was laid? What kind of curse it might have been?

Assuming trauma from the incident, the staff tried a slow, empathetic approach.

But to every question, the response was the same:

Detainee refused to answer any and all questions.

That’s when the staff began to suspect the worst.

Behavior interpreted as an attempt to deliberately avoid the glass prison’s interrogation effects.

And so, from the evening of Day 2, the true interrogation began.

Interrogation Intensity: 3.

Professional interrogator assigned.

Persistent, layered questioning. Trick questions. Subtle cues hidden in casual conversation. Even silence was treated as an answer.

As the days went by, the intensity rose.

By the end of Day 3, even a detainee skilled at resisting every probe was beginning to show signs of fracture.

Interrogation Intensity: 7.

Subject’s body restrained. Head and eyes fixed in place to prevent averted gaze.

Silence treated as affirmation.

To stop attempts at avoidance through meaningless vocalizations, subject was gagged during questioning.

Eventually, interrogators could discern basic yes/no answers.

Thankfully, the prison had not yet used environmental pressure—such as altering light, temperature, or comfort—to escalate the interrogation.

This was a compassionate call, considering the detainee’s health.

But also, ineffective.

Day 5.

Subject Condition: Poor.

Refused all sleep and meals.

The goblin fire assigned to the subject requested release on the detainee’s behalf, saying they were “pitiful.”

Denied—out of concern for escape or incident.

Instead, to preserve emotional stability, a few items favored by the goblin were permitted into the cell.

Subject expressed thanks.

Special Note – Interrogator’s Personal Opinion:

At this point, I believe they may be under a second curse.

I recommend immediate transfer to a specialized facility for full ritual exorcism.

But when directly asked, the subject responded clearly: “No.”

The prison’s mechanisms confirmed this as truth.

“Could be they’ve been suggested to believe they’re not cursed.”

Despite that theory, the transfer was put on hold.

The agent turned the page.

More dates. More declines in condition.

Day 6.

Subject Condition: Poor.

Provided with comfort meal (mung bean ginseng chicken soup). Left untouched overnight.

No answers in interview.

A proposal was made to transfer subject to the Nightmare Labyrinth for deeper interrogation.

Strongly opposed by field agents. Rejected.

“......”

“That’s the record up to yesterday.”

And from the next page on, the content shifted from documentation to inference.

Speculations about the detainee’s true identity.

Stamped with “For Authorized Personnel Only – Strictly Prohibited from External Reproduction.”

Accessible only by the current case officer and the interrogator.

Signs indicating possible spy activity confirmed.

The agent clenched his fist tightly.

Though the subject has maintained silence throughout all questioning, based on the truths extracted in extreme conditions, we are able to deduce several “questions the subject is most desperate to avoid.”

As follows:

Source of their information

True purpose of working at the Paranormal Disaster Response Agency

Identity of the being who placed the curse

Unexplained pre-employment gaps in background

Traces of multiple contaminations

And thus, a conclusion was drawn.

But the file didn’t end there.

However, given that the subject shows no known moral deficiencies, has performed all assigned paranormal disaster response duties with integrity, and displays verified ethical behavior, we believe this may be a case of coercion or supernatural manipulation.

Due to the limitations of yes/no-style interrogation, a new approach will be introduced to elicit more cooperative behavior.

The agent flipped the page.

It was the final one.

A temporary appendix document—the very reason he was here now.

Special Note:

An agent from Hyunmu Team 1 who transported the subject initially volunteered to act as interrogator over the past few days.

Request denied due to violation of protocol.

However, a different Hyunmu Team 1 agent who was also on-site during the incident has now volunteered.

Approval: Pending.

“......”

“Preparations are complete, Agent-nim.”

Ryu Jaegwan let the file drop from his hand.

And stepped toward the front of the glass orb—

To begin the interrogation.

I blinked.

A transparent ceiling came into view.

Surrounding me in this tight, hemispherical cell were a bed, desk, chair, a few books, and a set of meals placed with mechanical precision.

Beyond that, the air was fogged like a dream, but I could tell—this cell’s walls were clear.

“...So this is it.”

The Glass Prison.

Long ago, a goblin beat a man named Mr. Kim in a game of yut, winning a grand tiled mansion.

But unwilling to give up his home, Mr. Kim accused the goblin of cheating with trickery, slandering him and throwing him out.

Enraged, the goblin created a place where trickery was impossible—a space the size of a fingernail, inside a single glass bead.

He invited Mr. Kim back for a rematch.

And this time, the goblin won again.

Again and again, until he took everything.

House, wealth, family, appearance, name—even his lifespan.

And when the goblin, now having become “Mr. Kim,” stood triumphant in his mansion, he tossed his old cave and the glass bead behind as a gift.

Mr. Kim—no, the one who was now nothing—remained in that cave.

And even now, it is said that the “nothing” guards the Crystal Cave, making sure that no one cheats again.

The Paranormal Disaster Response Agency had weaponized that story—

Constructing the Glass Prison.

Where supernatural tricks don’t work.

Where illusions, deceit, or anomalous power all become inert.

A place patrolled by the mysterious gatekeeper.

“They even mixed in some other death-related anomaly, didn’t they...”

Whatever. It didn’t matter.

The point was:

In here, only what is clean and true remains.

Within this space, supernatural entities can exist,

but they cannot act.

No impossible feats. No miracles.

Like reality outside the Wiki.

Items were useless. Tattoos just ink.

They even confiscated my glass hand cannon during the search—

Not that it mattered. My inventory tattoos didn’t activate here anyway.

It meant, in a way... this was the safest place to be.

“At least I won’t suddenly fall into a ghost story here.”

Physically, as Choi Agent had said, this place was secure.

Some even volunteered to be locked up here to escape curses or misfortune.

But—

“......”

I couldn’t sleep.

My whole body pulsed with tension and surrender, in equal parts.

Just like the past few days.

The Glass Prison’s interrogation never stops.

Even as I clung to silence, more and more had already been extracted.

“They’ve probably figured it out by now.”

That I’m suspicious. Maybe even a spy.

And if so...

They might soon resort to even harsher methods.

The Disaster Response Agency—

was nothing if not ruthless toward confirmed threats.

I remembered the interrogation techniques I’d read about.

Cold sweat beaded at my temples.

“Damn it.”

Maybe Agent Choi had hoped I’d confess everything once the curse was lifted.

Maybe he expected me to give up—knowing I couldn’t return to Director Ho.

But... my secrets don’t end there.

There are things I know that can’t be explained away as simple espionage.

Secrets like:

My true origin

The Dream Incubator

Contaminations

And the full contents of the Dark Exploration Logs

“If I were going to talk, I should’ve done it at the start—kept it limited to the spy angle.”

But if I had...

What about Yeong-eun or Heo-un?

What about my wish ticket?

“Back then... it was the right choice.”

...Still.

Even I knew I wouldn’t last much longer.

“......”

I sank into exhaustion but forced myself to stay alert.

Soon, the next interrogation session would begin.

The questions. The traps. The bait.

I forced my nerves to settle.

Went over the list in my mind of things I must never reveal.

And then—

Crrrrrkkkkk.

The sound of glass shifting.

Someone was approaching from beyond the transparent wall.

The interrogator.

But it wasn’t the same handler from yesterday.

“...Detainee 37-999.”

“...!!”

Agent Ryu Jaegwan.

Cheongdong Agent.

Stood beyond the glass, his expression unreadable.

“Wait.”

Cheongdong is today’s interrogator?

The method had changed.

I tensed, trying to recalibrate my expectations.

Cheongdong Agent didn’t look away.

Slowly, deliberately, he spoke:

“...Before we begin, you are hereby informed: You may lie—but all falsehoods will be revealed.”

I knew that.

In the Glass Prison, all lies are reflected as their opposite truths.

Only silence can protect you.

— Excerpt from a detainee from Baekilmong.

That was why I hadn’t spoken.

Lying here was the same as confessing.

But... my refusal to speak was suspicious too.

They’d likely already guessed I knew how the prison works.

And they’d be right.

Even I would’ve suspected me by now.

I looked at Cheongdong Agent and clenched my jaw.

“They’re using someone I know. A familiar face, to get a reaction.”

A more humane method, sure—but...

Also effective.

“I will now begin the interrogation.”

I swallowed hard.

And then—

He asked a question I had never expected.

“Where is your home?”

“...!”

Chapter 203

Home.

A destination.

The reason I want the Wish Ticket.

What I call, in shortened form, the powerful desire to escape this world and return to the place I originally lived.

“I want to go home.”

...But only internally.

I never said it out loud.

And yet suddenly, in the middle of interrogation, that deeply personal matter was tossed out into the open.

“He’s asking where my home is?”

I unclenched my fingers, forcing myself not to grip the edge of the chair.

I can’t lie.

“Why are you suddenly asking me that?”

“...I heard that you consider ‘home’ to be important.”

From who? No—wait.

.........

I remembered.

—“So what’s your wish, then?”

—“Going home.”

Choi Agent must have told him.

I remembered that hellish night—my encounter with Director Ho in the taxi.

And the conversation I’d had with Choi Agent afterwards.

“......”

Home.

So it had been deemed a topic unrelated to my status as a spy, categorized under personal dialogue?

“Even so... why bring it up out loud like this?”

It was a risky move.

And just because Cheongdong Agent knew that didn’t change anything.

I tried to deflect.

“If I told you where I commute from, would that count?”

“No. That’s not a home.”

...!

Cheongdong looked at me silently.

“I know you’ve been staying at a motel.”

“......”

“And that the room has no personal furniture—barely anything that could be called your own. ...Just a few sets of clothes.”

It felt like my most guarded thoughts had been exposed.

“That’s not a home. In fact, you’ve deliberately left it as an impersonal space. You never applied for the agency’s staff dormitory either. ...Why?”

A question I couldn’t answer.

“Podo Agent.”

“......”

“I won’t insist you answer what I’m about to ask. But I want you to know this.”

Cheongdong spoke clearly, from behind the glass.

“This conversation is just between you and me.”

“......”

“No recording. No separate documentation. Only I will hear this.”

I looked up.

Our eyes met. He gave a slight nod and then set his file down.

Then said—

“I never had a home.”

“...!”

“My parents divorced when I was young, and after my mother passed away, I was raised in a group home.”

Wait—what?

“Even though I lived and slept there, I never thought of it as a home. When I heard the word ‘home,’ I’d think of the old villa where I lived with my mom. I can barely remember it now, just the feeling of it.”

His personal history poured into my ears—details that weren’t on any Wiki.

Not just a character.

Cheongdong Agent.

A real person’s story.

“Then one day, I got caught in a supernatural disaster on my way to school. That’s how I first encountered the agency. I asked someone how to work there, and they laughed.”

Later, after explaining his circumstances, the agency offered him a safe, part-time job for students—at a strange little bookstore they used.

“I worked there while attending night classes. And after I completed university, I took the civil service exam... and passed.”

Cheongdong looked at me.

“That’s how I came to be here.”

“......”

“And now, when I hear the word ‘home,’ I think of Hyunmu Team 1’s waiting room. Of the dorm room at the agency. I think of people’s faces.”

Ah.

“So I wanted to ask you.”

He asked quietly:

“...Podo Agent. Does your home... no longer exist in this world?”

“...!”

“Did you lose it?”

The implied phrase that followed:

‘Like I did.’

“......”

I wasn’t restrained like during previous interrogations.

I could have avoided the question freely.

But—

“Yes.”

“...!”

“I don’t have one.”

A flicker of emotion passed through Cheongdong’s eyes.

“Is that why you worked at Baekilmong—because you wanted to get it back?”

“It’s a little different.”

I chose my words slowly.

“I want to return to it.”

“......”

Cheongdong seemed to chew on the meaning of that.

Then—

“Podo Agent.”

His voice came firm.

“You cannot return to a place that no longer exists.”

“......”

“There are only those who will try to deceive you into thinking you can. ...Don’t let yourself be used like that. You’re stronger than that.”

You’re wrong.

I knew that much.

The Wish Ticket is real.

I know what you’re trying to say, but it doesn’t apply to—

“You can build a new home.”

“......!”

“Just like I did.”

I looked at him like I’d been struck in the head.

“If you go out there and meet good people, find a place where your heart feels at ease... somewhere you can rest, sleep in peace—”

Cheongdong declared:

“That place will be your home.”

The way he thought of the Disaster Agency’s waiting room as his.

A truth spoken from experience.

Cool and precise, like clear water.

So—

“I’m not asking you to believe it right away. But... I’d like you to consider it.”

“......”

“Could you?”

I nodded slowly.

And the glass showed nothing.

Truth.

A quiet smile spread across Cheongdong’s face.

“Good. Today’s session ends here.”

“......”

“I hope you get some real rest tonight.”

I can’t believe myself.

What did I just agree to even consider?

But the mood was inexplicably gentle.

And my heart felt... more at peace than I’d expected.

As if I hadn’t made the wrong choice.

Cheongdong didn’t press further.

“And please make sure to eat and sleep properly. I’ll be assigned as your interrogator for the next week, so no aggressive questioning for now. Just treat this time as a break.”

He added that if I needed anything, he’d try to get it—within safe limits, of course.

There was no trace of deceit.

It didn’t look like he was about to dump me into a Nightmare Labyrinth anytime soon...

“...Phew.”

For days, I’d been picking only the safest food, fighting to stay awake at all times, dodging every psychological trap.

Now all at once, my exhaustion nearly surged free.

The relief was almost dizzying.

But just then—

“And this is... a personal question.”

He hesitated a little.

“...Do you resent Choi Agent?”

“......!!”

“You’d be justified in feeling that way. They sent you to the glass prison the moment your geas was lifted. ...Without consulting a single person.”

A flicker of anger crossed Cheongdong Agent’s voice, quickly suppressed.

‘Looks like they fought over it.’

But not wanting to show it, Cheongdong smoothly shifted the topic.

“Still, Choi Agent didn’t lock you up and then leave you to rot for the past week.”

He went on to explain that Choi had repeatedly applied to take the role of interrogator—just to speak with me.

But—

“He was the one who requested your transfer to the prison. And when he applied to be your first-day interrogator, he failed the objectivity scoring. That’s why it was blocked.”

“...!”

“Luckily, I passed. So today... I was thinking about what I should say.”

Cheongdong Agent bowed his head slightly.

“I feel a little relieved... that you answered.”

“......”

“Then, I’ll see you tomorrow.”

With another reminder to rest and take care of my health, Cheongdong disappeared into the fog beyond the glass.

And just like that, I was once again alone with the dokkaebi flame inside the glass prison.

But the usual tension that came with hyper-vigilance wasn’t what filled my head.

It was more like being hit in the back of the skull. A dumbstruck haze wrapped around me.

And I replayed it all.

That conversation just now.

“Live here?”

Give up going home?

His suggestion had been: What if you built a new home here and settled down instead?

As a disaster response agent.

‘Was that a bait tactic for the interrogation?’

But considering Cheongdong Agent’s personality, it seemed unlikely he would go along with that kind of deceitful manipulation.

Which meant... he was being genuine.

With one unspoken condition.

The Disaster Management Bureau had not ruled me an “evil party.”

And...

They wanted me to stay.

“......”

Holy shit.

‘This situation might... be better than I thought.’

If Cheongdong was the interrogator, then maybe—just maybe—he’d let me confess only the spy part and stop there. I might get out of this prison with all my other secrets still intact.

And then, maybe...

Maybe I could just keep working.

“......”

Keep living as a Disaster Management Bureau agent.

Stay with Hyunmu Team 1, do good work with good people, continue just like that.

And if it ever became too much—if I admitted I was scared—they might even transfer me to a different team.

So I could carve out a safe, peaceful daily life even in this nightmare-world of ghost stories.

‘It’s... not the worst idea?’

No—in fact, it was starting to sound... nice.

Building a new home...

‘No.’

I slapped my own thigh hard enough to make a noise.

I couldn’t afford to let fatigue cloud my judgment and make me choose what was easiest.

Cheongdong only made that suggestion because he assumed I’d lost my home to some accident or disaster.

I couldn’t afford to let myself be mistaken.

‘My home is still there.’

Getting back to it is hard, yes.

But I’m not deluded. I’m not clinging to something impossible.

I can do this. I can go home—

...Besides.

I knew the truth.

Choosing to stay permanently in this world of <Dusk: The Record of Explorations into the Dark>?

Not a wise decision.

Do you remember what it said beneath that wiki title?

Prophecy of the End: Dusk

That’s right.

‘I can’t stay here too long.’

Because this world is, by design...

—Oh dear.

A smooth and pleasant male voice.

“You seem overwhelmed, friend.”

“...!!”

I jerked upright.

A hallucination?

“Hallucination? With a voice this crisp and charming? Please! Though, I suppose it’s understandable. Anyone would miss Brown after all this time. I don’t blame you!”

That fluid cadence was far too real to be my imagination.

I immediately reached into my jacket pocket and pulled it out.

A small rabbit plush doll.

“......”

A cold shiver crawled down my spine.

‘How is it talking?’

All supernatural phenomena are sealed inside the glass prison—

Oh.

‘They said supernatural beings can still “exist,” didn’t they.’

Just like the dokkaebi flame cowering on the wall right now.

So... “My Good Friend” wasn’t just a talking plush doll.

It had been recognized as an entity—a being—via its medium.

“Exactly! We’ve shared many delightful chats in your daily life, haven’t we? You can’t call that trickery. That’d be heartbreaking. My dear Deer! How cruel!”

The cheery tone of the host faltered briefly.

Like it realized something.

“...Yes... I’m hurt.”

.......

“It seems you’re not pleased to see me, friend. Even though it’s been a whole week.”

“I thought you’d be happy, like a child waiting all week for their favorite cartoon to air...”

“......”

So then.

This plush had been intentionally silent for a whole week—just like that?

“There you go again. Calling me a ‘plush doll.’”

A chill zipped down the back of my neck.

“Hm. Well. I suppose it doesn’t matter. A great show host must accept all titles—nicknames, slurs, you name it! That’s the price of fame. Especially when it comes from a dear friend!”

The host’s voice echoed in the crystal chamber.

Like a mic with too much reverb.

“Alright then. Since my friend has had such a rough week, how about a show-stopping performance?”

“Surprise! Look up, my friend!”

My head moved stiffly.

And right there, across the glass wall where Cheongdong Agent had stood—

“Let me introduce... ‘How to Escape the Glass Prison!’”

A video began playing.

Projected like a screen.

“...!”

“Better than that cartoon we were gonna watch, isn’t it? Oh ho—surprised? It’s fine. Supernatural phenomenon? I haven’t the faintest idea what you mean. Watching TV is the most natural, ordinary thing in the world!”

“Now then—”

“Let’s watch, shall we?”

Chapter 204

A video spills across the glass wall.

Unbelievable! I’m trapped in a bizarre prison! Where even is this place?

Aha, this is the Crystal Cavern in ■■ City? Thanks, Brown!

What? You’re even going to explain how to escape? Wow, thanks! We really are the best of friends!

It’s a miniature-style retro animation.

The protagonist, dressed exactly like I am right now, chats with a rabbit plush identical to mine.

Something’s wrong.

Something’s really wrong...

I turn my head. But the video plays there too.

I see! You break open the surrounding glass marbles to kill the other prisoners and use them as bait to lure out the Warden!

‘...!’

The video follows my gaze, no matter which way I look.

It plays from every single glass surface.

What an easy way out!

You’re amazing, Brown!

The rabbit plush, same size as the main character, helpfully flits through the space, explaining the escape method.

How to move as a sealed glass orb.

How to torture the Warden.

Maps and routes of escape.

It lays everything out in shocking detail, narrating viciously cruel methods with the tone of an educational children’s show.

...Like instructions from a preschool cartoon.

Ah.

“That’s right! Isn’t it just your style, Deer? The immersion is working beautifully. Splendid!”

The animation—now taken to an extreme childlike art style—shows blood, screams, the Warden’s severed limbs, and Disaster Management Bureau staff fleeing in panic.

It ends with the ‘main character’ and the rabbit plush Brown giving each other a high-five.

That’s it! That’s how you escape!

The final line echoes in my ears—

And then: applause.

Clap clap clap.

The light, crisp sound of gloved hands.

“Phew! A short, but intense journey, wasn’t it? So, friend—are you ready to escape, just like Mini-Brown showed you?”

“......”

It was loud, flashy, and retro—

Which somehow made the TV-style video even creepier.

Classic Brown.

But—I noticed something.

“Oh, don’t worry! Like I said—watching TV is the most normal, everyday thing there is!”

No.

“Hmm?”

I looked at the shadow of the rabbit plush.

‘You didn’t actually break the rules of the Glass Prison.’

“Oh.”

Because—

‘The video was played from outside the glass prison.’

It was merely projected inward.

Just like when I spoke to Agent Cheongdong through the glass wall.

‘You copied that.’

That’s how it bypassed the rules.

You made it feel like something was happening inside the prison, when it wasn’t.

‘All to make the presentation more shocking...’

After a brief silence—

“My goodness... You’re right, friend! Your sharp eyes and keen deductions—surely a nightmare for any magician!”

“A truly magnificent talent. Really, it makes me wistful...”

“But that’s not the point right now, is it? I haven’t heard your review of the program yet!”

“......”

“Well? What did you think?”

I opened my mouth.

‘I had a question.’

“Oh? A question about the escape method?”

“No.”

That’s not it.

The thing I really wanted to ask...

‘How much of my thoughts do you actually know?’

......

“Ah.”

“Only as much as I need to, to be a good friend!”

“...Thought so.”

That was the answer I expected.

Ever since the Late-Night Talk Show incident, I’d known—

This ‘host’ could read a disturbing amount of what was in my head.

Maybe even information I only accidentally thought about.

The escape video we just watched?

It was probably built on exactly that—my own knowledge.

But...

‘That’s all it is, huh.’

“.......”

“Deer. I detect a troubling implication in your words... No, no—surely it’s just my imagination. You’re not saying this Brown doesn’t understand you well enough, are you?”

“No.”

A faint smirk tugged at my lips.

“You understand exactly what you need to—for your own purposes.”

You’re picking and choosing.

Based on your most important criterion.

‘Entertainment.’

The chipper voice returned.

“What could be more important for an entertainer!”

“Besides, this was part of your promise too, wasn’t it? I clearly remember—you swore to entertain me...”

“Yeah. But...”

‘I... was mistaken about something.’

“A mistake? Could you explain what you mean by that?”

I held back a sigh and said it aloud.

I could finally admit it.

‘I unconsciously thought you’d put me first before the fun of the show.’

That you’d think about my situation.

That you’d care.

And that was absurd.

What was I expecting from a ghost-story-world talk show host?

But... we’d shared so many secrets.

Gone through so many crises together.

We’d even agreed to escape together.

And somewhere in all that, some irrational part of me started believing.

That feeling you get when you convince yourself someone will care, just because they’ve been around long enough.

I even forgot the line I always used to convince you:

“For the betterment of the show.”

That was your language, not mine.

But I’d let it slip.

Because every time—

You showed up.

You helped me.

You made it through with me.

And I became biased.

Toward this ‘Good Friend.’

I’d started trusting you, irrationally, by default.

‘I won’t anymore.’

Of course, even saying that doesn’t mean much.

I just need to be more careful.

But still...

I’ll say this much.

‘You said you were hurt, right? Well, me too.’

......

“...My word.”

A soft sigh came from the host.

“So that was it... I did suspect. But now I understand.”

“Deer—you were deeply disappointed when I refused to help you a few days ago.”

Whew.

“What a tragic misunderstanding! But all I wanted was to help my friend the best way I could. Let’s think this through again...”

The host’s voice dropped to a low murmur.

“If I had just helped you ‘normally’ back then, what would have happened?”

“You would’ve vanished, like magic. No context. No meaning. Just gone.”

This is insane.

“All the story you’ve built up until now—it would’ve just ended. Abruptly. Pointlessly. A finale so hollow it might as well not exist.”

“Is that... really what you wanted, Deer? No, right?”

“That’s...”

Just then, the dokkaebi flame that had been huddled on the wall floated over to me.

...Huh?

“What is it?”

I carefully picked it up in one hand.

It hovered, then, with trembling effort, stretched out toward the rabbit plush—forming a single gesture.

A thumbs-down.

“......”

Then it nuzzled into my side—

(Hiding its face, I think.)

“...Oh dear.”

‘Don’t get mad. That was funny. You knew the risk.’

“......”

‘Sorry. But isn’t this kind of what you would say, too?’

It was probably reckless to be this blunt.

But... I felt a little clearer afterward.

‘Anyway—that’s all I wanted to say. I’m not saying you were wrong.’

And I expected this kind of reaction.

So... I won’t be disappointed again.

I’ll just be content knowing there’s someone I can talk to, someone who might help, if the odds are right.

“...Friend. You don’t believe in me anymore.”

“...Fine. Every relationship has misunderstandings. I suppose this, too, is something I must endure...”

There were a few footfalls—unpleasant in their clipped cadence—but they soon faded away.

That was... a little surprising.

I didn’t think he’d hold back.

“Because there’s something more important right now, maybe.”

There’s something far more urgent than squabbling among the parties involved, you see. Watching my friend continue to be left in this cramped and stifling place is not, I must say, a very delightful sight.

Escape.

Have you made up your mind?

Hm.

I stood up from my seat.

The small, round solitary cell—a single glass orb—is visible.

And now, strangely enough, the method for escaping this infamous Glass Prison is etched into my mind, as though engraved.

If I did it like that, it would undoubtedly be classified as an irregular incident in the Dark Exploration Records.

But....

.......

No.

I thought about it.

And I made my decision.

To choose the best possible action I could take right now.

Surely you don’t mean to go through with the interrogation as is?

.......

I do.

I sat back down in the chair.

“...I’ll go through the interrogation.”

“......”

Agent Choi lowered his head.

Prisoner ID: 37-999

Prisoner: Kim ■■ (Agent name: Podo)

Affiliation: Dispatch & Rescue Squad, Hyeonmu Team 1 (Suspended)

The updated interrogation records were in his hand.

As he flipped page after page, a grueling week’s worth of documentation lay before him.

Refusal to testify. Deteriorating health. Increasingly extreme interrogation protocols.

But beyond that stretch of agony, something changed.

Like this:

Day 8

Prisoner status: Poor (Recovering)

Noticeable change in behavior following the previous day's interrogation.

Significant reduction in uncooperative reactions—no longer deliberately inducing audiovisual disturbances or maintaining silence.

Sleep and meal patterns approaching recommended levels.

Observed caring for dokkaebi flame and plush doll (referred to as “friend”).

Attributed to new interrogator with personal ties.

That was after Ryu Jaegwan began his visits.

Personal report from staff:

Prisoner appears to be stabilizing. Rather than increasing interrogation pressure, a natural confession approach is likely more effective.

This assessment was agreed upon by the majority.

More pages followed.

Day 9

Prisoner status: Poor (Recovering)

Special meal (short rib soup) permitted. Interrogator brought identical meal to eat with the prisoner during the session.

Prisoner cooperated with questioning.

Acknowledged that signs of contamination were from separate supernatural disasters prior to their Disaster Management Bureau employment.

The prisoner confined in the Glass Prison began to share more and more.

Hints of deeper conversations with the interrogator emerged throughout the report.

Personal history, personality traits, affection for the Bureau, fear of supernatural phenomena, even feelings of relief and fulfillment when rescuing civilians.

And then, eventually....

It arrived here.

Day 12

Prisoner admitted to formerly working for ■■■ Corporation.

Confirmed that there had been external pressure in applying to the Bureau, and that separate objectives were to be achieved during employment.

However, also confirmed that a nearly-deadly geumje (binding curse) had been cast.

Pronounced no intent to harm the Bureau.

Confirmed: no actual damage to the Bureau during their tenure.

Pronounced truth: prisoner desires to continue working for the Bureau regardless of initial intent.

Interrogator reported the testimony to higher authorities.

“......”

Agent Choi stilled the tremor in his hand and turned the final page.

Day 13

Prisoner status: Stable

And then:

Testimony verified by interrogation protocols.

Prisoner 37-999 (Agent name: Podo) has had their detainment lifted.

Authorization granted to reinstate agent status.

Release scheduled for the following day.

And that day... was today.

Clack.

Agent Choi closed the folder.

His tired expression flickered with tension and relief in equal measure.

“They’ll be out soon.”

“Yeah.”

“Please stop pacing.”

“I know.”

And yet, Agent Choi kept pacing around the waiting room.

Because in his head, the next steps were already taking shape.

“This isn’t the end.”

The spy’s status had been all but confirmed.

A number of difficult procedures would follow.

But those would be internal disciplinary matters—the kind handled only for Bureau agents.

Kim Soleum would now undergo that process as a member of Hyeonmu Team 1.

“That means I can help.”

That was what mattered.

Now the Glass Prison administrator would retrieve the orb that had held Agent Podo, release the geumje, and transfer custody to them.

Before any formal penalty could begin, there were things they had to talk about—about Baekilmong Corporation, about the secrets and items Soleum had been hiding, about the strange abilities he might possess....

But that could be handled slowly.

The most important thing was that the interrogation had ended.

And that Kim Soleum had stabilized.

It was a long road back, but they’d made it.

Agent Choi finally exhaled a breath of relief.

The past two weeks had been soul-crushing.

He didn’t know how many lines he’d crossed to get to this point.

And....

“...Jaegwan.”

“......”

“Thank you. You did a lot.”

Agent Cheongdong gave a small nod.

It was a stiff gesture, but Agent Choi caught the quiet offer of reconciliation behind it.

He looked at his junior agent warmly.

A smile touched his lips.

A mixture of guilt and gratitude.

“First thing—once all this is done, the three of us are going to a hot spring. I’m paying. No excuses.”

“Yes, sir.”

Just as Agent Choi patted Ryu Jaegwan on the back—

Knock knock.

“...!”

The door opened, and the administrator appeared.

“Detainment has been lifted.”

“Then...”

“Yes. Agent Podo is currently waiting in the prep room.”

“Thank you.”

Agent Choi shook the staffer’s hand quickly and moved with purpose.

“W-Wait—!”

He already knew the location.

Without hesitation, he made his way into the cavern depths toward a steel door with a nameplate.

Soleum would be inside.

“......”

...What to say first? He still had no clue.

To be honest, he still didn’t even know who Soleum truly was.

What the hell was that?

That strange laboratory. That unnatural form. That entity.

But one thing he was sure of:

That night, under the curse—

Kim Soleum had tried to save him.

Even at the risk of dying.

“......”

“Then I’ll just have to figure the rest out now.”

Without hesitation, Agent Choi flung the door open.

And—

There was no one inside.

“......”

The empty prep room showed no signs of human presence.

Like no one had ever been there to begin with.

“Agent?”

Agent Choi stood frozen.

Then turned back to look inside.

On a steel chair lay something starkly out of place.

A neatly folded sheet of paper.

On the front was a single word:

Resignation.

Agent Choi picked it up.

A few small items tumbled to the floor—

a googly-eyed candy, a stick-type syringe.

They looked like junk, but each one... clearly an item.

“......”

He froze again, then slowly unfolded the paper.

Inside, written in pressed, deliberate handwriting:

Hello, Agent.

I wanted to properly apologize for everything that has happened.

I’m truly sorry.

—Agent Podo.

Chapter 205

The letter was calm.

I’m writing this to note down the things I never got to say.

It felt like a note left behind by someone going away for a while.

Kim Soleum wrote about the spare glasses he’d left on the couch in Hyeonmu Team 1’s waiting room, about the flat rear tire on the bicycle out front of the main office, about the printer ink that was still in transit.

Too trivial and fragmentary to be called a handover.

Hints of lingering attachment seemed to rise, only to vanish again by the next paragraph.

But that unassuming tone, steady throughout, eventually slipped—almost unnoticed—into something quietly significant.

Things no new agent of the Disaster Management Bureau could ever know.

...Advice regarding supernatural disasters.

_In the ironing section of the laundry shop at the four-way intersection, wearing silk garments will no longer be safe after the rescue of the woman in the blue dress.

Please make sure no agent wears anything like that in._

These were urban legends Hyeonmu Team 1 had investigated.

It read as if Soleum had pieced things together himself—but there was an odd, certain tone that made it feel like he’d already known.

Still, it clearly wasn’t written with detachment.

It was written with concern and care.

Trembling eyes scanned quickly through the lines.

And then...

Once again, I’m sorry.

The content shifted into something more personal.

The night the geumje (binding curse) had been placed was described in extremely vague terms—only the person involved would be able to understand it.

As if to spare someone who might still be cursed.

Then followed apologies and gratitude to Agent Cheongdong, who had made it possible to speak the truth honestly, without coercion or pressure.

And then—

Still, I want to go home.

A farewell.

I can’t give up without even trying to return.

But because of that desire, I feel like I’ve wasted the Disaster Management Bureau agents’ time.

I’m sorry again—for failing to live up to expectations, for not accepting punishment properly, for quitting like I was running away.

“......”

I know this might be an inconvenience, but I’ve included some of the items I found most useful.

If Hyeonmu Team 1 can make use of them, I’d be glad.

He had detailed the abilities and effects of the items he’d left behind.

And at the very end—

_Just in case, I’ll add this.

The writing I left on the whiteboard in Hyeonmu Team 1’s waiting room doesn’t mean anything.

I was worried you might hesitate to erase it, wondering if it was a hint or a sign.

But you can wipe it off without concern.

It was just something I left on impulse. There’s no deeper meaning._

The letter ended.

The agent reading it hastily flipped the paper over.

...There, on the bottom corner of the back, was one more line in small handwriting.

Thank you for everything.

Please stay safe and be happy.

“......”

The one reading the letter lowered their gaze—to the items scattered on the floor.

Then, slowly, bent down and picked one of them up.

It looked like a cheap candy.

But it was an item—one that could briefly restore the body to its state in a “better time.”

Something extremely useful in the field of rescue dispatch.

The bits and pieces left behind glittered faintly on the ground.

“......”

“......”

Agent Choi raised his head.

The sunken eyes of the two Hyeonmu Team 1 agents met each other.

If he had just now vanished, there was a good chance he hadn’t left the premises yet.

“Wait—w-wait, agents...!”

They rushed out of the prep room with the startled administrator in tow.

To begin a full search of the crystal cave.

And—

“......”

Standing motionless in the corner of the room, Kim Soleum quietly followed behind them.

With a single sheet of paper in his hand.

Through the gap in the door they had opened to enter the room.

An elegant and courteous exit. Not bad, this method.

“......”

I quietly looked up at the sunlight pouring down.

So I made it out.

I tried to finish this as safely and gently as I could, but... honestly, I don’t know.

I don’t know how the Disaster Management Bureau will take it.

Or what Hyeonmu Team 1 will think.

As I walked faster, trying not to recall the faces I’d glimpsed in passing, I kept my pace steady.

Out of the crystal cave.

To the bus stop.

To the express train.

And I didn’t forget to thank the host who had kindly dimmed the lights for me.

Oh, think nothing of it!

Thanks to that, I was able to leave quietly like this.

If he hadn’t helped, I would’ve had to escape in a much messier, more emotionally taxing way....

“......”

[This train will depart shortly....]

I tried not to think.

How thoroughly they’d search the crystal cave after I left,

how they’d handle the items I left behind,

how suspicious the Bureau would become of me,

whether Hyeonmu Team 1 would erase the words I’d written on their whiteboard....

Instead, I tried to sleep.

The express train headed for Seoul, thankfully, didn’t evoke the fear of the Tamna-bound line.

I finally managed to doze off for a bit before waking.

And to avoid any risk of being followed, I picked a random motel and paid in cash.

Thud.

Once the door closed behind me, I took a moment to breathe and begin my inventory check.

Only then did reality start to return.

“...I left almost everything behind.”

Just a few consumable items remained.

Especially the ones from the Space Shopping Mall—guaranteed safe items—I’d left most of those behind.

...Because I probably wouldn’t need them anymore.

“People who are more likely to get hurt should use them instead.”

What I had left were a few pieces of equipment, and my tattoos.

But....

Fwoosh.

A small light flickered out from inside my clothes.

A dokkaebi flame.

“You’re really going to keep following me?”

The flame danced up and down, clearly affirming.

This guy had stuck with me all the way to Seoul.

Even left behind the glass lantern it had been housed in.

I’d worried about being tracked, so in a way, this was less risky, but still....

“They’ll be worried. You’re part of the Bureau.”

The flame flashed brightly—like it was arguing back.

It says it’s old enough to make its own decisions.

Goodness me. What a stubborn, foolish creature.

Sigh.

According to Brown’s reluctant interpretation, the dokkaebi fire, though slow, is capable of recharging itself on its own.

It apparently recovers faster if exposed to the energy of a sacred lightning bolt—but... where would I even find something like that?

Anyway, the dokkaebi fire’s will seemed as firm as ever.

“...Then I’ll be counting on you for just a bit longer.”

The flame gleamed happily and floated a lap around the motel room.

......To be honest, it wasn’t just inconvenient.

At least one thing stayed with me.

A trace of where I once belonged.

Proof of the days I spent with good people... that kind of thing.

Just a little longer. I’ll hold on to it.

It wouldn’t be long, anyway.

After stroking the dokkaebi fire once, I let it transform into my right arm.

You’re free again, Mr. Nohru! You can go anywhere now. So—where to?

Strictly speaking, it’s actually the opposite, isn’t it?

If the Supernatural Disaster Management Bureau doesn’t put out a wanted alert, I’ll be lucky, and to the Baekilmong Corporation, I’m already a dead man.

There weren’t many places I could call or show up at.

Not to mention,Agent Choi already found out about the dream cultivation chamber at the Euphoric Research Lab.

Even if the binding curse stops me from revealing the chamber to the Bureau, he himself could visit at any time. I’d need to prepare precautions—but I had to stay cautious.

Still, I didn’t break out without a plan.

I’ve got one.

Oh?

...The problem was that it was a deeply uncomfortable plan.

“......”

I activated the phone that I had sealed inside my tattoo.

A flood of unread messages from the last two weeks.

Good thing nothing came up during the interrogation.

I’d come close to exposing both Ms. Yeongeun and Mr. Heoun’s identities.

The moment I got caught in Jisan Village, I shoved the phone into my tattoo—and inside the glass prison, where tattoos stopped functioning, that had been a hidden blessing.

I exhaled in relief and scrolled through the messages.

Mostly spam.

But among them—several were from someone I knew.

[Hey]

[You okay?]

[When will you tell me what you found out]

[Hey]

[If you’re sick]

[If anything’s wrong, reach out]

It was the security team captain.

“......”

Strangely, it made me feel weird.

With this person, I always seemed to be... the one receiving concern and help.

Come to think of it, I haven’t contacted him since the curse.

After some hesitation, I replied.

[Sorry. Something came up. I’m fine now.]

And after another moment of conflict, I sent one more.

[Thanks again for checking into that thing.

Would it be okay to meet tomorrow or the next day to talk about it?

And maybe get something good to eat.]

He read it fairly quickly, but his reply came slowly.

[ᄋᄏ]

“......”

That’s settled.

Now it was time to go do what needed doing.

I chose my destination.

I didn’t like it, but I didn’t have a better option.

And...

I was already prepared.

[Please prepare the wish voucher.]

[I’ve found the information you wanted.]

To Director Ho.

“Soleum-nim, you’ve returned!”

“......”

I lifted my head.

There, at the table in the familiar waiting area of the Fox Counseling Office, sat Director Ho with a smile.

My stomach churned on reflex.

...A visceral aversion to even having to make this decision.

A strange sense of guilt—as if I were betraying someone who had broken the curse for me.

And at the same time, a contradictory feeling: that now, with the curse lifted, I could finally have a proper negotiation.

“I’m here to receive my wish voucher.”

“Just a moment. There’s something I want to say first.”

Still wearing that gentle smile, Director Ho hesitated, as if embarrassed.

It made my skin crawl even more.

“The curse about Soleum-nim’s death—I may have gone a little overboard with that. Looking back, I think it was a rather emotional decision on my part... pretty shameful, huh.”

“......”

“It’s a good thing it’s gone now, right?”

I recalled the strange ritual that the General of Mt. Daecheong had performed.

And Director Ho’s grotesque reaction through Deputy Eunhaje’s body during that rite.

...Even if I’m curious or worried about the deputy, I shouldn’t ask.

If Director Ho senses any familiarity or concern from me, that might end up harming Deputy Eunhaje.

My insides twisted.

So instead of nodding easily, I answered with caution.

“It doesn’t matter. Curse or not, a promise is a promise. I’m here to keep the one I made with you.”

“Really? That’ll be tough.”

“......”

“You don’t even know what kind of ‘extinction-class disaster’ I was asking for, do you, Soleum-nim?”

That’s true.

Director Ho had said he’d tell us more once we spies gained access to the secret archives beneath Gangwon-do.

But....

I knew.

I’d already figured out what he’d been planning to demand.

“I deduced it.”

“......”

“Just tell me the disaster’s name. If it doesn’t match the information I brought, I’ll accept punishment.”

After all, there was no risk to him now.

He could curse me again, punish me, whatever.

With the situation changed, there was no longer a fellow agent to undo it for me.

So....

“Alright.”

I figured he would.

Director Ho laced his fingers together and smiled.

“The name of that supernatural disaster is: Saekwang Special City.”

“......”

“Oh, and don’t say it out loud outside the Fox Counseling Office. You’ll regret it.”

Director Ho looked into my eyes.

“So?”

“......”

“Does it match what you brought?”

I didn’t look away.

“Yes.”

“...!”

“Here.”

Thud.

A document was placed on the table of the Fox Counseling Office’s waiting area.

“This is the file on the extinction-class supernatural disaster you just mentioned.”

“......”

“Please, verify it.”

Director Ho reached out, picked up the paper, and began reading.

And....

“......!”

He closed it.

Expression wiped clean, he stared at me.

“...How did you find this out?”

So it was the correct answer.

“That’s not important.”

Because in truth—

It wasn’t something I had found out.

I already knew it.

That’s right.

This was why I’d said I could bring him the information within a month.

Because I already knew it.

The fastest and most certain method—

To write down the information from The Dark Exploration Log.

It had been the last resort, one I’d avoided out of fear—because there was no way to convince or explain it to other spies, and if I raised Director Ho’s suspicion, the consequences could be terrifying.

I’d wanted to go about it as safely as possible.

But with the whole board overturned, there was only one move left.

I had to act fast.

If I took too long, the Bureau would begin searching for other spies.

If Yeongeun or Heoun got caught, they’d never get their wish voucher and would suffer doubly, bound by Director Ho’s curse....

I had to strike first.

And yet....

“I’m sure you were in the Glass Prison just yesterday. Where could you have possibly found this?”

“......”

“They don’t keep anything like that in there.”

Correct.

The Glass Prison stored no high-level intel.

Occasionally, a report would be written based on information obtained during interrogation, but nothing more.

But....

That’s exactly why they keep some office supplies on hand for report writing.

Like these.

Unmarked Bureau report templates used for documenting supernatural disasters, with the branch field redacted—because they’re written in an undisclosed location.

The perfect format for printing secret, extinction-class disaster documents stored in the sealed archive.

Ironically, getting thrown into the Glass Prison had given me the exact medium I needed.

And so, I was able to lay this document before Director Ho.

The Saekwang Special City file I created, based entirely on what I read on the wiki.

“......”

But there was no need to tell him that.

It would only endanger me.

I did come with a few final precautions...

Cold sweat beaded down my back.

But still—I had to say it.

“What matters is that I brought the promised information. And in the process, I did receive help from the other two.”

“......”

“So now, Director, it’s your turn to fulfill the agreement.”

A smile spread across Director Ho’s face.

“Very well.”

“...!”

“Congratulations, Soleum-nim. You are now officially receiving your wish voucher.”

I clenched my fist.

Chapter 206

Wish Ticket.

The most certain way for me to return home in this bizarre world of urban legends, where strange bad endings sprouted up everywhere.

It was for this, and this alone, that I endured working for Baekilmong Corporation—and even endured becoming a spy.

And now, finally, after fulfilling the conditions to receive it, Director Ho lightly clapped his palms in front of me.

As if applauding me.

Clap, clap, clap...

"I'm genuinely happy that you're finally receiving a Wish Ticket."

"......."

"But... haven't you ever thought about it?"

The clapping stopped.

"What if Director Ho never intended to give you the Wish Ticket in the first place?"

"......."

"He could just keep changing the conditions. Tell you to do something else, and something else again, dangling it forever like a bait."

Director Ho smiled gently.

"Have you really never thought about that?"

"I have thought about it."

You’d have to be a fool not to.

In society, there were countless cases where even after you completed a job, they would delay or cheat you out of payment.

Conversely, there were also plenty of cases where someone would take the payment first and then never properly do the job.

And in this situation, the Wish Ticket was Director Ho’s one-sided promise.

And promises made by the strong could always be broken. Because there were no penalties for breaking them.

But—

"Even so, I know that Director Ho values promises very highly."

"......."

The Taboos.

Director Ho was a kind of entity that bound others through ‘promises’—a type of monster.

Yeah. By now, thinking of Director Ho as a human would be the foolish thing.

And for monsters, rules are a critical element.

From the perspective of urban legend logic, it was probably to create a horrifying atmosphere and to give victims false hope of escape before crushing them...

Regardless, that meant the rules could actually be more trustworthy than if Director Ho were just some evil human.

There was a much higher probability he would have no choice but to keep his promise.

Especially when remembering how unnaturally adamant Director Ho had been about the reliability of the counselor in the 'Fox Consultation Room.'

Even if he hid other intentions or laid traps, he wouldn’t make a false promise.

I stared directly at Director Ho’s expressionless face.

"Therefore, I judged that anything you promised with your own mouth would absolutely be fulfilled."

"......."

Clap.

"Of course!"

Director Ho clapped his hands and laughed.

"I always keep my promises. I never make promises I can’t keep... absolutely."

"......."

"Well then."

Director Ho lifted a briefcase he had placed under the table and reached inside.

"Time for you to receive your Wish Ticket."

And then, something dazzling came out.

A small glass vial.

“...!!”

In the waiting area of the Fox Consultation Room—that cozy, warm place—an enchanting, otherworldly aurora-like glow began to shimmer.

It was overwhelming, unbelievable that something like that could emanate from a single tiny bottle.

Wish Ticket.

"This is yours, Soleum-nim."

"......."

Clink.

"Go ahead."

I reached out my hand.

Just as my arm was about to touch the Wish Ticket resting on the table—

"Ah, wait just a moment."

Director Ho smiled lightly.

"It’s not just one bottle, you know?"

“...!!”

Clink. Clink.

Still smiling, Director Ho pulled out two more glass vials from the briefcase, both swirling with that same ecstatic, iridescent light, and placed them on the table.

A total of three vials.

Holy shit.

Across the tabletop, the waves of a milky-way-like brilliance rippled like an ocean made of pearls.

"...This is,"

"Yes. These are the Wish Tickets for the two other employees who worked with you. You did say, didn’t you? That the two of them also contributed enough to deserve it."

That’s right.

But... for him to hand them over so easily?

And Director Ho wasn’t finished.

"However, since you’re the one who came to the reporting session as the team representative, it only makes sense for you to receive them as the representative!"

"......!"

Wait a second.

"Are you saying... I’m supposed to accept all three bottles... and then hand one to each of them?"

"Well."

...!

"You reported as a team, right? Then the distribution should be at the discretion of the representative, shouldn’t it?"

"......."

"I won’t interfere."

A dark flame flickered briefly in Director Ho’s eyes.

But in the next instant, it was gone, replaced by his usual gentle smile.

"I sincerely hope that, with this Wish Ticket, the deepest wish hidden in your heart will come true, Soleum-nim."

I opened my mouth.

"Director, aren’t you going to use it yourself?"

"......."

Director Ho gazed at me for a moment.

"Unfortunately, it’s an item that only regular employees can use."

"......."

"Executives, sadly, are contract workers. That’s the rule."

Director Ho’s hand stretched out slightly across the table, as if he wanted to touch the Wish Ticket—then withdrew.

"And so."

"......."

"Will you be taking them? Or would you prefer to come back for them later? I’m fine with either."

Take all three bottles now.

Or leave them all behind.

But realistically, there was only one choice I could make.

"Yes. I’ll take them."

I nodded.

And then—

"Excellent!"

Director Ho beamed and pulled out a box from his briefcase, carefully beginning to package the vials one by one.

"The instructions for using the Wish Ticket are enclosed inside! I’ll pack each bottle individually!"

Like a professional salesperson, he neatly packed the vials into the box, tied a ribbon around it, and slipped it into a paper bag, pushing it across the table toward me.

"Here you go."

"...Thank you."

I picked up the bag.

For a moment, a strange feeling of déjà vu brushed past me.

...I did it.

It was finally starting to feel real—that I had it in my hands now.

It was inside this black paper bag.

I had obtained the Wish Ticket.

"Have a safe trip back, Soleum-nim. I’ll contact you again soon."

My head felt hazy.

I rose from my seat.

And after bowing politely to Director Ho, who waved me off, I exited the waiting area of the Fox Consultation Room.

Creaaak.

From behind me, I thought I heard the door to the Consultation Room open and close—

As if someone else had entered.

And when I turned my head at the exit, there was already no one at the table anymore.

All that remained were the three Wish Ticket vials in my hands.

"......."

—Oh, congratulations, friend! Now, do you just have to make a wish and blow out the candles?

If only it were that easy.

The real problem is... I have two extra bottles in my hands.

A little later.

At a nearby café.

How the hell do I hand these over?

I sat at a table, clutching the bag, my brain racing frantically.

It felt like walking around with a bag full of billion-won checks.

Every time I remembered the Wish Tickets sitting inside, I had the urge to pull one out and just drink it immediately—but I had things I needed to do first.

First and foremost—

I need to contact Ko Yeongeun.

I just had to figure out a way to pass the item to her indirectly.

If I could just manage that, she could fulfill her wish and leave safely. She wouldn’t have to step into any more ghost stories.

And—

I could save Jang Heoun too.

As long as someone wished for Jang Heoun’s revival using the Wish Ticket, it would be done.

Everything was perfect.

Once the distribution was finished...

I could finally go home.

My heart pounded with anxiety and anticipation.

I reached into the bag, brushing my hand against the box inside, focusing on what task I needed to complete first.

First, a safe place—

Bzzz!

My smartphone vibrated and a message popped up.

[Today]

[Where should I g0?]

"......."

The Guard Captain.

We had arranged to meet after sunset today.

I was originally supposed to get information from him.

But now, I didn’t need information anymore.

Whether it was Yukhoe Research Lab or the source of Baekilmong—it wouldn't matter once I got out of here.

But in this world of urban legends, leaving meant...

It also meant I would never see anyone I met here ever again.

"......."

After a moment of hesitation, I sent a text.

[I'd like to meet you here.]

And that evening.

"Hmm...."

I met the Guard Captain at a corner table of a famous donut shop, one that stayed open late.

I had prepaid for some donuts earlier—since most of them would sell out during the day—and picked them up now.

Seeing the donuts, the Guard Captain’s exhausted face lit up just a little.

"Thanks...."

"Not at all. I should be thanking you for all the help you've given me. Though... something like this hardly seems enough to repay you."

"No.... It's plenty."

With a slightly happier face, the Guard Captain eagerly grabbed and began eating the donuts.

And then, completely casually, he dropped a bombshell.

"About that information you wanted... Yukhoe Research Lab... it's still under our company building...."

"......!!"

Wait.

"What do you mean?"

"Just what I said... hmm."

The Guard Captain looked at me.

"You’ve actually seen it too...."

I have?

"Hmm... when we first met... when you got sucked into that scroll... you went down to the bathroom on Basement Level 2, remember...?"

"...!"

"Don’t you remember?"

I do.

When I had gone down there to chase the whereabouts of Assistant Manager Lee Byungjin, who had disappeared....

There had been a strangely normal-looking office hallway stretched out in front of me.

—Shouldn’t a place like this have proper safety gear or facilities...?

I clearly remembered thinking that.

And then—

—Miss Roe?

Yeah.

That was when the nice plushie friend suddenly started talking...!

That bizarre underground office at Baekilmong...

It was inside an urban legend.

And that legend—was Yukhoe Research Lab?

"How... did you find out?"

"Hmm... even we only knew the codename... didn’t really know... until we found some unredacted documents."

Shrugging his shoulders, the Guard Captain continued.

"Anyway, even if you go further down... it's just more of the same office corridors... repeating endlessly...."

"...Exactly the same corridors?"

"Yeah. Identical offices... layout, even the scratches and marks are the same. It’s definitely the same place. But... somehow... the time feels a little different each time."

The sounds of people chatting, shadows holding meetings, employees packing things, people fighting, or even dust-covered ruins—

Every time you took the elevator down another floor, the scenery shifted slightly, like a game of spot-the-difference.

Endlessly.

"...How deep does it go?"

"We don’t know... hmm. I only went as far as... I think it was the 23rd floor down...? Below that, it was blocked off...."

"......."

"I think there was a security team that went down to the 40th floor once...? But they all died... hmm."

"......."

It was a chilling description.

Those offices...

A cold shiver ran down my spine.

Why was Yukhoe Research Lab down there? Why was it fused into an urban legend? What kind of insane project had Baekilmong built their company over?

Questions flickered across my mind.

But soon, I realized—none of it mattered.

Because I was going to leave.

"......."

"Ah."

Apparently, the Guard Captain sensed that from my expression too.

"I guess... you don't need this anymore."

"......."

He pulled his cap lower over his eyes.

"Hmm, if that's the case..."

And with the keen instinct of someone who worked for Baekilmong, he easily guessed the most plausible reason for someone suddenly losing all interest in everything.

"Did you... win the Wish Ticket?"

"......."

I hesitated for a moment, but in the end, I nodded.

A faint smile rose on the Guard Captain’s lips.

A pure smile, just congratulating me.

"That’s good...."

"......."

"Um, you know how to use it, right?"

"...Yeah."

At my answer, the Guard Captain nodded along, hesitated briefly, then asked:

"So... this time... you’re really resigning?"

A fair question—since he knew that I hadn’t actually quit before, but had merely faked it to keep working under Director Ho.

I nodded.

"I see...."

"......."

The Guard Captain lowered his head.

"Um... after you leave... could we maybe... still talk sometimes...?"

"I would like that too."

But we couldn’t.

I averted my gaze.

"Because of the nature of my wish... I don't know what will happen."

"...Ah."

The Guard Captain fell silent.

"A world-changing... wish?"

"...Yes."

"Then... it can’t be helped."

"......."

So he knew after all.

Yeah, if he had been a captain in the Elite Team, it would be much more natural for him to know—

especially since the more field agents cared about Wish Tickets, the more likely they were to know the truth about them.

The nature of Wish Tickets.

—You want tips for using a Wish Ticket?

There aren’t any. Just drink it and make your wish.

It’ll come true.

...At least from your point of view.

—From the advice of Deputy C of the Elite Team.

This was how it worked.

If a user wished to become younger, they would become younger.

If they wished for 10 billion won to be cleanly deposited into their bank account without any tax problems, it would happen.

The Wish Ticket potion worked like a magic lamp that could change the state of the individual user.

However—

What if the wish was something more... macroscopic?

Wishes that went beyond merely altering the user’s personal state.

Say, if you wished for a past moment:

"I want to go back to when I lived happily with my parents as a child."

"I want to go back to the moment my husband got into the car accident and stop him."

Or something larger:

"Please destroy the world."

"I wish everyone would become kind."

"I want superconductors to be invented."

Wishes that sought to change the very world itself.

Even in those cases, the potion activated.

Your wish would be granted. However—

Only from your own perspective.

In other words—

You would be moved to a world where that wish had been fulfilled.

The Wish Ticket was an incredibly personal potion—

its power was a one-person omnipotence.

The person who fulfilled their wish would disappear from the world.

Sometimes they stayed—but that was only if they hadn’t made their wish correctly, or if the wish hadn’t been grand enough to require such a shift.

In any case, the world itself would not undergo massive change because of a Wish Ticket.

Only the person would move.

That’s why the Guard Captain knew too.

If my wish was something that changed the whole world,

then it meant I would vanish.

Disappear completely.

"......."

I looked at the Guard Captain, who had finished eating the donuts.
